In the selection and use of his cabinet Lincoln employed the strengths of his opponents in a manner that emboldened his presidency. Lincoln commented on his thought process, "We need the strongest men of the party in the Cabinet. We needed to hold our own people together. I had looked the party over and concluded that these were the very strongest men. Then I had no right to deprive the country of their services."[\[314\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoodwin2005319-324) Goodwin described the group in her biography as a *[Team of Rivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Team_of_Rivals "Team of Rivals")*.[\[315\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoodwin2005-325)

There were two measures passed to raise revenues for the federal government: tariffs (a policy with long precedent), and a [federal income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax_in_the_United_States "Income tax in the United States"). In 1861, Lincoln signed the second and third [Morrill Tariffs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morrill_Tariff "Morrill Tariff"), following the first enacted by Buchanan. He also signed the [Revenue Act of 1861](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revenue_Act_of_1861 "Revenue Act of 1861"), creating the first U.S. income tax—a flat tax of 3 percent on incomes above $800 (equivalent to $27,129 in 2023[\[316\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-inflation-US-326)).[\[317\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDonald1996424-327) The [Revenue Act of 1862](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revenue_Act_of_1862 "Revenue Act of 1862") adopted rates that increased with income.[\[318\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPaludan1994111-328)

The Lincoln Administration presided over the expansion of the federal government's economic influence in other areas. The [National Banking Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Banking_Act "National Banking Act") created the system of national banks. The U.S. issued paper currency for the first time, known as [greenbacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenback_\(1860s_money\) "Greenback (1860s money)")—printed in green on the reverse side.[\[319\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-329) In 1862, Congress created the [Department of Agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Agriculture "United States Department of Agriculture").[\[317\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDonald1996424-327)

In response to rumors of a renewed draft, the editors of the *[New York World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_World "New York World")* and the *[Journal of Commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Journal_of_Commerce "The Journal of Commerce")* published a false draft proclamation that created an opportunity for the editors and others to corner the gold market. Lincoln attacked the media for such behavior, and ordered a military seizure of the two papers which lasted for two days.[\[320\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDonald1996501%E2%80%93502-330)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg/38px-Wikisource-logo.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Wikisource-logo.svg)

[Wikisource](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikisource "Wikisource") has original text related to this article:

**[Thanksgiving Proclamation (1863)](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Thanksgiving_Proclamation_\(1863\) "wikisource:Thanksgiving Proclamation (1863)")**

Lincoln is largely responsible for the [Thanksgiving holiday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thanksgiving_\(United_States\) "Thanksgiving (United States)").[\[321\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDonald1996471-331) Thanksgiving had become a regional holiday in New England in the 17th century. It had been sporadically proclaimed by the federal government on irregular dates. The prior proclamation had been during [James Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Madison "James Madison")'s presidency 50 years earlier. In 1863, Lincoln declared the final Thursday in November of that year to be a day of Thanksgiving.[\[321\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDonald1996471-331)

In June 1864 Lincoln approved the Yosemite Grant enacted by Congress, which provided unprecedented federal protection for the area now known as [Yosemite National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yosemite_National_Park "Yosemite National Park").[\[322\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-332)

### Supreme Court appointments

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Abraham_Lincoln\&action=edit\&section=29 "Edit section: Supreme Court appointments")]

| Justice                                                                                                                  | Nominated        | Appointed        |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------- | ---------------- |
| [Noah Haynes Swayne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noah_Haynes_Swayne "Noah Haynes Swayne")                              | January 21, 1862 | January 24, 1862 |
| [Samuel Freeman Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Freeman_Miller "Samuel Freeman Miller")                     | July 16, 1862    | July 16, 1862    |
| [David Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Davis_\(Supreme_Court_justice\) "David Davis (Supreme Court justice)") | December 1, 1862 | December 8, 1862 |
| [Stephen Johnson Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Johnson_Field "Stephen Johnson Field")                     | March 6, 1863    | March 10, 1863   |
| [Salmon Portland Chase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salmon_P._Chase "Salmon P. Chase") (Chief Justice)                 | December 6, 1864 | December 6, 1864 |

Lincoln's philosophy on court nominations was that "we cannot ask a man what he will do, and if we should, and he should answer us, we should despise him for it. Therefore we must take a man whose opinions are known."[\[321\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDonald1996471-331) Lincoln made five appointments to the Supreme Court. [Noah Haynes Swayne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noah_Haynes_Swayne "Noah Haynes Swayne") was an anti-slavery lawyer who was committed to the Union. [Samuel Freeman Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Freeman_Miller "Samuel Freeman Miller") supported Lincoln in the 1860 election and was an avowed abolitionist. David Davis was Lincoln's campaign manager in 1860 and had served as a judge in the Illinois court circuit where Lincoln practiced. Democrat [Stephen Johnson Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Johnson_Field "Stephen Johnson Field"), a previous California Supreme Court justice, provided geographic and political balance. Finally, Lincoln's Treasury Secretary, Salmon P. Chase, became Chief Justice. Lincoln believed Chase was an able jurist, would support Reconstruction legislation, and that his appointment united the Republican Party.[\[323\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlue1987245-333)

### Foreign policy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Abraham_Lincoln\&action=edit\&section=30 "Edit section: Foreign policy")]

Main articles: [Presidency of Abraham Lincoln § Foreign policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Abraham_Lincoln#Foreign_policy "Presidency of Abraham Lincoln"), and [Diplomacy of the American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomacy_of_the_American_Civil_War "Diplomacy of the American Civil War")

Lincoln named his main political rival, William H. Seward, as Secretary of State and left most diplomatic issues in Seward's portfolio. However, Lincoln did select some top diplomats as part of his patronage policy.[\[324\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-334) He also closely watched the handling of the [Trent Affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trent_Affair "Trent Affair") in late 1861 to make sure the situation did not escalate into war with Britain.[\[325\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-335) Seward's main role was to keep Britain and France from supporting the Confederacy. He was successful after indicating to Britain and France that the Union would declare war on them if they supported the South.[\[326\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-336)

## Assassination

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Abraham_Lincoln\&action=edit\&section=31 "Edit section: Assassination")]

Main article: [Assassination of Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Abraham_Lincoln "Assassination of Abraham Lincoln")

[![Painting of Lincoln being shot by Booth while sitting in a theater booth.](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Lincoln_assassination_slide_c1900_-_Restoration.jpg/220px-Lincoln_assassination_slide_c1900_-_Restoration.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lincoln_assassination_slide_c1900_-_Restoration.jpg)

An illustration of [Lincoln's assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Abraham_Lincoln "Assassination of Abraham Lincoln") on April 14, 1865, in the presidential booth at [Ford's Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ford%27s_Theatre "Ford's Theatre"), featuring (left to right): assassin [John Wilkes Booth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Wilkes_Booth "John Wilkes Booth"), Abraham Lincoln, [Mary Todd Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Todd_Lincoln "Mary Todd Lincoln"), [Clara Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clara_Harris "Clara Harris"), and [Henry Rathbone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Rathbone "Henry Rathbone"), published in 1900

[John Wilkes Booth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Wilkes_Booth "John Wilkes Booth") was a well-known actor and a Confederate spy from Maryland; though he never joined the Confederate army, he had contacts with the Confederate secret service.[\[327\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDonald1996586%E2%80%93587-337) After attending Lincoln's last public address, on April 11, 1865, in which Lincoln stated his preference that the franchise be conferred on some black men, specifically "on the very intelligent, and on those who serve our cause as soldiers",[\[328\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-338) Booth hatched a plot to assassinate the President.[\[329\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison20103%E2%80%934-339) When Booth learned of the Lincolns' intent to attend a play with General Grant, he planned to assassinate Lincoln and Grant at [Ford's Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ford%27s_Theatre "Ford's Theatre"). "Booth had attended a dress rehearsal the day before to better rehearse his scheme for shooting Lincoln ... and then escaping."[\[330\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-340) Lincoln and his wife attended the play *[Our American Cousin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_American_Cousin "Our American Cousin")* on the evening of April 14, just five days after the Union victory at the [Battle of Appomattox Courthouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Appomattox_Courthouse "Battle of Appomattox Courthouse"). At the last minute, Grant decided to go to New Jersey to visit his children instead of attending the play.[\[331\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDonald1996594%E2%80%93597-341)

At 10:15 in the evening, Booth entered the back of Lincoln's theater box, crept up from behind, and fired at the back of Lincoln's head, mortally wounding him. Lincoln's guest, Major [Henry Rathbone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Rathbone "Henry Rathbone"), momentarily grappled with Booth, but Booth stabbed him and escaped.[\[332\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDonald1996597Martin2010-342) After being attended by [Doctor Charles Leale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Leale "Charles Leale") and two other doctors, Lincoln was taken across the street to [Petersen House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petersen_House "Petersen House"). After remaining in a [coma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coma "Coma") for nine hours, Lincoln died at 7:22 in the morning on April 15.[\[333\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESteers2010153-343)[\[k\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-348) Stanton saluted and said, "Now he belongs to the ages."[\[338\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDonald1996598%E2%80%93599,_686-349)[\[l\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-350) Lincoln's body was placed in a flag-wrapped coffin, which was loaded into a hearse and escorted to the White House by Union soldiers.[\[339\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-351) President Johnson was sworn in later that same day.[\[340\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-352)

Two weeks later, Booth, refusing to surrender, was tracked to a farm in Virginia. He was mortally shot by Sergeant [Boston Corbett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Corbett "Boston Corbett") and died on April 26. Secretary of War Stanton had issued orders that Booth be taken alive, so Corbett was initially arrested to be court martialed. After a brief interview, Stanton declared him a patriot and dismissed the charge.[\[341\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESteers2010153Donald1996599-353)

### Funeral and burial

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Abraham_Lincoln\&action=edit\&section=32 "Edit section: Funeral and burial")]

Main article: [State funeral of Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_funeral_of_Abraham_Lincoln "State funeral of Abraham Lincoln")

From April 19 to 21, the late President lay in state, first in the [East Room](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Room "East Room") of the [White House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House "White House") and then in the [Capitol Rotunda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Capitol_rotunda "United States Capitol rotunda"). The caskets containing Lincoln's body and the body of his third son [Willie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Wallace_Lincoln "William Wallace Lincoln") then traveled for three weeks on the *Lincoln Special* [funeral train](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_funeral_of_Abraham_Lincoln#Funeral_train_to_Springfield,_Illinois "State funeral of Abraham Lincoln").[\[342\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTETrostel200231%E2%80%9358-354) The train followed a circuitous route from Washington D.C. to Springfield, Illinois, stopping at many cities for memorials attended by hundreds of thousands. Many others gathered along the tracks as the train passed with bands, bonfires, and hymn singing[\[343\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTETrostel200231%E2%80%9358Goodrich2005231%E2%80%93238-355) or in silent grief. Poet [Walt Whitman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Whitman "Walt Whitman") composed "[When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/When_Lilacs_Last_in_the_Dooryard_Bloom%27d "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd")" to eulogize him, one of [four poems he wrote about Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Whitman_and_Abraham_Lincoln "Walt Whitman and Abraham Lincoln").[\[344\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-356) African Americans were especially moved; they had lost their "[Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses "Moses")".[\[345\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHodes2015164-357) In a larger sense, the reaction was in response to the deaths of so many men in the war.[\[346\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHodes2015197%E2%80%93199-358) Historians emphasized the widespread shock and sorrow, but noted that some Lincoln haters celebrated his death.[\[347\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHodes201584,_86,_96%E2%80%9397-359) Lincoln's body was buried at [Oak Ridge Cemetery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oak_Ridge_Cemetery "Oak Ridge Cemetery") in Springfield and now lies within the [Lincoln Tomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Tomb "Lincoln Tomb").[\[348\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-360)

[![Lincoln sitting with his hand on his chin and his elbow on his leg.](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/AbrahamLincolnOilPainting1869Restored.jpg/170px-AbrahamLincolnOilPainting1869Restored.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:AbrahamLincolnOilPainting1869Restored.jpg)

*[Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln_\(Healy\) "Abraham Lincoln (Healy)")*, an 1869 portrait by [George Peter Alexander Healy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Peter_Alexander_Healy "George Peter Alexander Healy")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Abraham_Lincoln_O-116_by_Gardner%2C_1865.png/220px-Abraham_Lincoln_O-116_by_Gardner%2C_1865.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Abraham_Lincoln_O-116_by_Gardner,_1865.png)

Lincoln in February 1865, two months prior to his [assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Abraham_Lincoln "Assassination of Abraham Lincoln")

As a young man Lincoln was a [religious skeptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_skepticism "Religious skepticism").[\[349\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarwardine20034Wilson199984-361) He was deeply familiar with the [Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible "Bible"), quoting and praising it.[\[350\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDonald199648%E2%80%9349,_514%E2%80%93515-362) He was private about his position on organized religion and respected the beliefs of others.[\[351\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-363) He never made a clear profession of Christian beliefs.[\[352\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTENoll1992-364) Throughout his public career, Lincoln often quoted Scripture.[\[353\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-365) His three most famous speeches—[the House Divided Speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln%27s_House_Divided_Speech "Lincoln's House Divided Speech"), [the Gettysburg Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gettysburg_Address "Gettysburg Address"), and [his second inaugural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln%27s_second_inaugural_address "Abraham Lincoln's second inaugural address")—all contain direct allusions to Providence and quote from Scripture.

In the 1840s Lincoln subscribed to the [Doctrine of Necessity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Doctrine_of_Philosophical_Necessity_Illustrated "The Doctrine of Philosophical Necessity Illustrated"), a belief that the human mind was controlled by a higher power.[\[354\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDonald199648%E2%80%9349-366) With the death of his son Edward in 1850 he more frequently expressed a dependence on God.[\[355\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParrillo2000227%E2%80%93253-367) He never joined a church, although he frequently attended [First Presbyterian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Presbyterian_Church_\(Springfield,_Illinois\) "First Presbyterian Church (Springfield, Illinois)") in Springfield, Illinois, with his wife beginning in 1852.[\[356\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhite2009180-368)[\[m\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-369) When he was president, Lincoln and his wife often attended services at the [New York Avenue Presbyterian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Avenue_Presbyterian_Church "New York Avenue Presbyterian Church") in Washington, D.C.[\[357\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-370)

In the 1850s Lincoln asserted his belief in "providence" in a general way and rarely used the language or imagery of the evangelicals; instead, he regarded the republicanism of the Founding Fathers with an almost religious reverence.[\[358\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-371) The death of his son Willie in February 1862 may have caused him to look toward religion for solace.[\[359\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson1999251%E2%80%93254-372) After Willie's death, he questioned the divine necessity of the war's severity. He wrote at this time that God "could have either *saved* or *destroyed* the Union without a human contest. Yet the contest began. And having begun He could give the final victory to either side any day. Yet the contest proceeds."[\[360\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson1999254,_quoting_Lincoln,_Abraham,_\[httpsquodlibumichedullincolnlincoln51893rgndiv1sortoccursubviewdetailtypesimpleviewfulltextq1Yetthecontestbegan_%22Meditation_on_the_Divine_Will%22,_September_2,_1862?]-373)

Lincoln believed in an all-powerful God who shaped events and by 1865 was expressing that belief in major speeches.[\[352\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTENoll1992-364) By the end of the war, he increasingly appealed to the Almighty for solace and to explain events, writing on April 4, 1864, to a newspaper editor in Kentucky:

> I claim not to have controlled events, but confess plainly that events have controlled me. Now, at the end of three years struggle the nation's condition is not what either party, or any man devised, or expected. God alone can claim it. Whither it is tending seems plain. If God now wills the removal of a great wrong, and wills also that we of the North as well as you of the South, shall pay fairly for our complicity in that wrong, impartial history will find therein new cause to attest and revere the justice and goodness of God.[\[361\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-374)

This spirituality can best be seen in his second inaugural address, considered by some scholars[\[362\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-375) as the greatest such address in American history, and by Lincoln himself as his own greatest speech, or one of them at the very least.[\[n\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-376)[\[363\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-377) Lincoln explains therein that the cause, purpose, and result of the war was God's will.[\[364\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-378) Lincoln's frequent use of religious imagery and language toward the end of his life may have reflected his own personal beliefs or might have been a device to reach his audiences, who were mostly [evangelical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicalism "Evangelicalism") [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism "Protestantism").[\[365\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarwardine200327%E2%80%9355-379) On the day Lincoln was assassinated, he reportedly told his wife he desired to visit the [Holy Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Land "Holy Land").[\[366\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuelzo1999434-380)

## Health

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Abraham_Lincoln\&action=edit\&section=34 "Edit section: Health")]

Main article: [Health of Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_of_Abraham_Lincoln "Health of Abraham Lincoln")

Lincoln is believed to have had depression, [smallpox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smallpox "Smallpox"), and [malaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaria "Malaria").[\[367\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-381) He took [blue mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_mass "Blue mass") pills, which contained [mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercury_\(element\) "Mercury (element)"),[\[368\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-382) to treat [constipation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constipation "Constipation").[\[369\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-383) It is unknown to what extent this may have resulted in [mercury poisoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercury_poisoning "Mercury poisoning").[\[370\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-384)

Several claims have been made that Lincoln's health was declining before the assassination. These are often based on [photographs of Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_photographs_of_Abraham_Lincoln "List of photographs of Abraham Lincoln") appearing to show weight loss and muscle wasting.[\[371\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-theatlantic.com-385) It is also suspected that he might have had a rare genetic disease such as [Marfan syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marfan_syndrome "Marfan syndrome") or [multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiple_endocrine_neoplasia_type_2B "Multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2B").[\[371\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-theatlantic.com-385)

## Legacy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Abraham_Lincoln\&action=edit\&section=35 "Edit section: Legacy")]

See also: [Cultural depictions of Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_depictions_of_Abraham_Lincoln "Cultural depictions of Abraham Lincoln")

### Republican values

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Abraham_Lincoln\&action=edit\&section=36 "Edit section: Republican values")]

Lincoln's redefinition of *[republican values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism_in_the_United_States "Republicanism in the United States")* has been stressed by historians such as [John Patrick Diggins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Patrick_Diggins "John Patrick Diggins"), [Harry V. Jaffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_V._Jaffa "Harry V. Jaffa"), [Vernon Burton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vernon_Burton "Vernon Burton"), Eric Foner, and Herman J. Belz.[\[372\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas200861-386) Lincoln called the [Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Declaration_of_Independence "United States Declaration of Independence")—which emphasized freedom and equality for all—the "[sheet anchor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheet_anchor "Sheet anchor")" of republicanism beginning in the 1850s. He did this at a time when the [Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_United_States "Constitution of the United States"), which "tolerated slavery", was the focus of most political discourse.[\[373\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJaffa2000399Thomas200861-387) Diggins notes, "Lincoln presented Americans a theory of history that offers a profound contribution to the theory and destiny of republicanism itself" in the 1860 Cooper Union speech.[\[374\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiggins1986307Thomas200861-388) Instead of focusing on the legality of an argument, he focused on the moral basis of republicanism.[\[375\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoner2010215Thomas200861-389)

His position on war was founded on a legal argument regarding the Constitution as essentially a contract among the states, and all parties must agree to pull out of the contract. Furthermore, it was a national duty to ensure the republic stands in every state.[\[376\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJaffa2000263Thomas200861-390) Many soldiers and religious leaders from the north, though, felt the fight for liberty and freedom of slaves was ordained by their moral and religious beliefs.[\[377\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-391)

As a Whig activist Lincoln was a spokesman for business interests, favoring high tariffs, banks, infrastructure improvements, and railroads, in opposition to [Jacksonian democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacksonian_democrats "Jacksonian democrats").[\[378\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBorittPinsker2002196%E2%80%93198,_229%E2%80%93231,_301-392) Lincoln shared the sympathies that the Jacksonians professed for the common man, but he disagreed with the Jacksonian view that [the government should be divorced from economic enterprise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laissez-faire "Laissez-faire").[\[379\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECurrent1999-393) Nevertheless, Lincoln admired [Andrew Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Jackson "Andrew Jackson")'s steeliness as well as his patriotism.[\[380\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz2012-394) According to historian [Sean Wilentz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sean_Wilentz "Sean Wilentz"):[\[380\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz2012-394)

> Just as the Republican Party of the 1850s absorbed certain elements of Jacksonianism, so Lincoln, whose Whiggery had always been more egalitarian than that of other Whigs, found himself absorbing some of them as well. And some of the Jacksonian spirit resided inside the Lincoln White House.

[William C. Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_C._Harris_\(historian\) "William C. Harris (historian)") found that Lincoln's "reverence for the Founding Fathers, the Constitution, the laws under it, and the preservation of the Republic and its institutions strengthened his conservatism."[\[381\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris20072-395) James G. Randall emphasizes his tolerance and moderation "in his preference for orderly progress, his distrust of dangerous agitation, and his reluctance toward ill digested schemes of reform." Randall concludes that "he was conservative in his complete avoidance of that type of so-called 'radicalism' which involved abuse of the South, hatred for the slaveholder, thirst for vengeance, partisan plotting, and ungenerous demands that Southern institutions be transformed overnight by outsiders."[\[382\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERandall1962175-396)

### Reunification of the states

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Abraham_Lincoln\&action=edit\&section=37 "Edit section: Reunification of the states")]

[![Bureau of Engraving and Printing portrait of Lincoln as president](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/LINCOLN%2C_Abraham-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg/226px-LINCOLN%2C_Abraham-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:LINCOLN,_Abraham-President_\(BEP_engraved_portrait\).jpg)

[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:LINCOLN,_Abraham-President_\(BEP_engraved_portrait\).jpg "File:LINCOLN, Abraham-President (BEP engraved portrait).jpg")

[Bureau of Engraving and Printing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Engraving_and_Printing "Bureau of Engraving and Printing") portrait of Lincoln as president

In Lincoln's first inaugural address, he explored the nature of democracy. He denounced secession as anarchy, and he explained that majority rule had to be balanced by constitutional restraints. He said, "A majority held in restraint by constitutional checks and limitations, and always changing easily with deliberate changes of popular opinions and sentiments, is the only true sovereign of a free people."[\[383\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBelz199886-397)

The successful reunification of the states had consequences for how people viewed the country. The term "the United States" has historically been used sometimes in the plural ("these United States") and other times in the singular. The Civil War was a significant force in the eventual dominance of the singular usage by the end of the 19th century.[\[384\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-398)

### Historical reputation

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Abraham_Lincoln\&action=edit\&section=38 "Edit section: Historical reputation")]

> In his company, I was never reminded of my humble origin, or of my unpopular color.[\[385\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDouglass2008259%E2%80%93260-399)
>
> — [Frederick Douglass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Douglass "Frederick Douglass")

In [surveys of U.S. scholars ranking presidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States "Historical rankings of presidents of the United States") conducted since 1948, the top three presidents are generally Lincoln, Washington, and [Franklin Delano Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_Delano_Roosevelt "Franklin Delano Roosevelt"), although the order varies.[\[386\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-400)[\[o\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-402) Between 1999 and 2011, Lincoln, [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy "John F. Kennedy"), and [Ronald Reagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Reagan "Ronald Reagan") were the top-ranked presidents in eight [public opinion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_opinion "Public opinion") surveys, according to Gallup.[\[388\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-403) A 2004 study found that scholars in the fields of history and politics ranked Lincoln number one, while legal scholars placed him second after George Washington.[\[389\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTETarantoLeo2004264-404)

Lincoln's assassination left him a national martyr. He was viewed by abolitionists as a champion of human liberty. Republicans linked Lincoln's name to their party. Many, though not all, in the South considered Lincoln as a man of outstanding ability.[\[390\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChesebrough199476,_79,_106,_110-405) Historians have said he was "a [classical liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_liberal "Classical liberal")" in the 19th-century sense. [Allen C. Guelzo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allen_C._Guelzo "Allen C. Guelzo") states that Lincoln was a "classical liberal democrat—an enemy of artificial hierarchy, a friend to trade and business as ennobling and enabling, and an American counterpart to [Mill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Stuart_Mill "John Stuart Mill"), [Cobden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Cobden "Richard Cobden"), and [Bright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Bright "John Bright") (whose portrait Lincoln hung in his White House office)".[\[391\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-406)[\[392\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERandall196265%E2%80%9387-407)

Sociologist [Barry Schwartz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Schwartz_\(sociologist\) "Barry Schwartz (sociologist)") argues that Lincoln's American reputation grew slowly from the late 19th century until the [Progressive Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_Era "Progressive Era") (1900–1920s), when he emerged as one of America's most venerated heroes, even among white Southerners. The high point came in 1922 with the dedication of the [Lincoln Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Memorial "Lincoln Memorial") on the [National Mall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Mall "National Mall") in Washington, D.C.[\[393\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchwartz2000109-408)

Union nationalism, as envisioned by Lincoln, "helped lead America to the nationalism of [Theodore Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt "Theodore Roosevelt"), [Woodrow Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson "Woodrow Wilson"), and [Franklin Delano Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt "Franklin D. Roosevelt")."[\[394\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBorittPinsker2002222-409) In the [New Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal "New Deal") era, liberals honored Lincoln not so much as the [self-made man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-made_man "Self-made man") or the great war president, but as the advocate of the common man who they claimed would have supported the [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state "Welfare state").[\[395\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchwartz200823,_91%E2%80%9398-410)

Schwartz argues that in the 1930s and 1940s the memory of Abraham Lincoln was practically sacred and provided the nation with "a moral symbol inspiring and guiding American life." During the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression "Great Depression"), he argues, Lincoln served "as a means for seeing the world's disappointments, for making its sufferings not so much explicable as meaningful." Franklin D. Roosevelt, preparing America for war, used the words of the Civil War president to clarify the threat posed by Germany and Japan. Americans asked, "What would Lincoln do?"[\[396\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchwartz2008xi,_9,_24-411) However, Schwartz also finds that since World War II Lincoln's symbolic power has lost relevance, and this "fading hero is symptomatic of fading confidence in national greatness." He suggested that [postmodernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postmodernism "Postmodernism") and [multiculturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism "Multiculturalism") have diluted greatness as a concept.[\[397\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchwartz2008xi,_9-412)

In the [Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War "Cold War") years Lincoln's image shifted to a symbol of freedom who brought hope to those oppressed by [Communist regimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_regime "Communist regime").[\[395\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchwartz200823,_91%E2%80%9398-410) He had long been known as the Great Emancipator,[\[398\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-413) but, by the late 1960s, some African American intellectuals, led by [Lerone Bennett Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lerone_Bennett_Jr. "Lerone Bennett Jr."), denied that Lincoln deserved that title.[\[399\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-414)[\[400\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-415) Bennett won wide attention when he called Lincoln a [white supremacist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_supremacist "White supremacist") in 1968.[\[401\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBennett196835%E2%80%9342-416) He noted that Lincoln used ethnic slurs and told jokes that ridiculed blacks. Bennett argued that Lincoln opposed social equality and proposed that freed slaves voluntarily move to another country. The emphasis shifted away from Lincoln the emancipator to an argument that blacks had freed themselves from slavery, or at least were responsible for pressuring the government to emancipate them.[\[402\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECashin200261KelleyLewis2005228-417) Defenders of Lincoln retorted that he was a "moral visionary" who deftly advanced the abolitionist cause, as fast as politically possible.[\[403\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStriner20061-418) Brian Dirck stated that few Civil War scholars take Bennett (or [Thomas DiLorenzo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_DiLorenzo "Thomas DiLorenzo"))[\[404\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-419) seriously, pointing to their "narrow political agendas and faulty research".[\[405\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirck2009382-420)

By the 1970s Lincoln had become a hero to [political conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States "Conservatism in the United States")[\[406\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-421)—apart from [neo-Confederates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Confederates "Neo-Confederates") such as [Mel Bradford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mel_Bradford "Mel Bradford"), who denounced his treatment of the white South—for his intense nationalism, his support for business, his insistence on stopping the spread of slavery, his acting on [Lockean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockean "Lockean") and [Burkean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burkean "Burkean") principles on behalf of both liberty and tradition, and his devotion to the principles of the Founding Fathers.[\[407\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBelz2014514%E2%80%93518Graebner195967%E2%80%9394Smith201043%E2%80%9345-422) Lincoln became a favorite of liberal intellectuals across the world.[\[408\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-423)

Barry Schwartz wrote in 2009 that Lincoln's image suffered "erosion, fading prestige, benign ridicule" in the late 20th century.[\[409\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchwartz2008146-424) By contrast, David Herbert Donald opined in his 1996 biography that Lincoln was distinctly endowed with the personality trait of [negative capability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negative_capability "Negative capability"), defined by the poet [John Keats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Keats "John Keats") and attributed to extraordinary leaders who were "capable of being in uncertainties, Mysteries, doubts, without any irritable reaching after fact and reason".[\[410\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDonald199615-425)

In the 21st century President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama "Barack Obama") named Lincoln his favorite president and insisted on using the [Lincoln Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Bible "Lincoln Bible") for his inaugural ceremonies.[\[411\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-426)[\[412\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-427)[\[413\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-428)

Lincoln has often been portrayed by Hollywood, almost always in a flattering light.[\[414\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-429)[\[415\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-430)

Lincoln has also been admired by political figures outside the U.S., including [German political theorist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Address_of_the_International_Working_Men%27s_Association_to_Abraham_Lincoln "Address of the International Working Men's Association to Abraham Lincoln") [Karl Marx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Marx "Karl Marx"),[\[416\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-431) Indian [independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_independence_movement "Indian independence movement") leader [Mahatma Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi "Mahatma Gandhi"),[\[417\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-432) former [Liberian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberia "Liberia") president [Ellen Johnson Sirleaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellen_Johnson_Sirleaf "Ellen Johnson Sirleaf"),[\[418\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-433) leader of the Italian [Risorgimento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Risorgimento "Risorgimento"), [Giuseppe Garibaldi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giuseppe_Garibaldi "Giuseppe Garibaldi"),[\[419\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-434) and Libyan revolutionary [Muammar Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muammar_Gaddafi "Muammar Gaddafi").[\[420\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-435)

### Memory and memorials

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Abraham_Lincoln\&action=edit\&section=39 "Edit section: Memory and memorials")]

Main article: [Memorials to Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorials_to_Abraham_Lincoln "Memorials to Abraham Lincoln")

Lincoln's portrait appears on two denominations of [United States currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_currency "United States currency"), the [penny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penny_\(United_States_coin\) "Penny (United States coin)") and the [$5 bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_five-dollar_bill "United States five-dollar bill"). He appears on postage stamps across the world.[\[421\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-c980-436)[\[422\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-437) While he is usually portrayed bearded, he did not grow a beard until 1860 at the suggestion of 11-year-old [Grace Bedell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grace_Bedell "Grace Bedell"). He was the first of five presidents to do so.[\[423\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECollea201813%E2%80%9314-438)

He has been memorialized in many town, city, and county names,[\[424\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDennis2018194-439) including the [capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln,_Nebraska "Lincoln, Nebraska") of Nebraska.[\[425\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDennis2018197-440) The United States Navy [*Nimitz*-class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nimitz-class_aircraft_carrier "Nimitz-class aircraft carrier") aircraft carrier [USS *Abraham Lincoln* (CVN-72)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Abraham_Lincoln_\(CVN-72\) "USS Abraham Lincoln (CVN-72)") is named after Lincoln, the second Navy ship to bear his name.[\[426\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-441) The [Lincoln Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Memorial "Lincoln Memorial") is one of the most visited monuments in the nation's capital[\[427\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-442) and is one of the most visited [National Park Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Park_Service "National Park Service") sites in the country.[\[428\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Atlantic_-_Nyce-443) Ford's Theatre, among the most visited sites in Washington, D.C.,[\[428\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Atlantic_-_Nyce-443) is across the street from [Petersen House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petersen_House "Petersen House"), where Lincoln died.[\[429\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-444) Memorials in Springfield, Illinois, include the [Abraham Lincoln Presidential Library and Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln_Presidential_Library_and_Museum "Abraham Lincoln Presidential Library and Museum"), [Lincoln's home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Home_National_Historic_Site "Lincoln Home National Historic Site"), and [his tomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Tomb "Lincoln Tomb").[\[430\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-445) A portrait carving of Lincoln appears with those of three other presidents on [Mount Rushmore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Rushmore "Mount Rushmore"), which receives about 3 million visitors a year.[\[431\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-446) A [statue of Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln:_The_Man "Abraham Lincoln: The Man") that influenced later sculptors[\[432\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-447) stands in [Lincoln Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Park "Lincoln Park"), Chicago, with recastings given as diplomatic gifts standing in [Parliament Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_Square "Parliament Square"), London, and [Parque Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_Lincoln "Parque Lincoln"), Mexico City.[\[433\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-448)[\[434\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-449)[\[435\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-450)

In 2019, Congress officially dedicated room H-226 in the [United States Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Capitol "United States Capitol") to Abraham Lincoln.[\[436\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-451) The room is located off [National Statuary Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Statuary_Hall "National Statuary Hall") and served as the [post office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post_office "Post office") of the House while then-Representative Abraham Lincoln served in Congress from 1847 to 1849.[\[437\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-452)[\[438\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-453)

2. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-7)** The identity of Lincoln's grandmother Bathsheba Herring, though without certainty, is the consensus of multiple Lincoln biographers. She was the daughter of Alexander and Abigail Herring (née Harrison).[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison1935276-6)
3. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-11)** Thomas, born January 1778, would have been 8 at the attack, May 1786. Older sources use six.[\[8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilsonDavisWilsonHerndon199835%E2%80%9336-10)
4. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-22)** Later, this land became part of a separate county in 1818.[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBartelt20083,_5,_16-21)
5. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-47)** Historians disagree on who initiated the move; Thomas Lincoln had no obvious reason to do so. One possibility is that other members of the family, including Dennis Hanks, may not have matched Thomas's stability and steady income.[\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBartelt200838%E2%80%9340-46)
6. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-65)** The Lincolns' last descendant, great-grandson [Robert Todd Lincoln Beckwith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Todd_Lincoln_Beckwith "Robert Todd Lincoln Beckwith"), died in 1985.[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-64)
7. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-115)** Lincoln was a descendant of the Harrisons through his grandmother, Bathsheba Herring.[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison1935280%E2%80%93286,_350%E2%80%93351-114)
8. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-136)** [Eric Foner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Foner "Eric Foner") contrasts the abolitionists and anti-slavery Radical Republicans of the Northeast, who saw slavery as a sin, with the conservative Republicans, who thought it was bad because it hurt [white people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_people "White people") and blocked progress. Foner argues that Lincoln was in the middle, opposing slavery primarily because it violated the [republicanism principles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism_in_the_United_States "Republicanism in the United States") of the [Founding Fathers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States "Founding Fathers of the United States"), especially the equality of all men and democratic self-government as expressed in the [Declaration of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Declaration_of_Independence "United States Declaration of Independence").[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoner201084%E2%80%9388-135)
9. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-138)** Although the name of the Supreme Court case is *Dred Scott v. Sandford*, the respondent's [surname](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surname "Surname") was actually "Sanford". A [clerk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_clerk "Court clerk") misspelled the name, and the court never corrected the error.[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-137)
10. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-223)** Major Northern newspapers, however, demanded more—they expected victory within 90 days.[\[213\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDonald1996295%E2%80%93296-222)
11. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-348)** At the moment of death some observers said his face seemed to relax into a smile.[\[334\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-344)[\[335\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-345)[\[336\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-346)[\[337\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-347)
12. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-350)** Other versions of the quotation have been offered, including "He now belongs to the ages," "He is a man for the ages," and "Now he belongs to the angels." Gopnik, Adam, "Angels and Ages: Lincoln's language and its legacy," [*The New Yorker*, May 21, 2007.](https://www.newyorker.com/magazine/2007/05/28/angels-and-ages)
13. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-369)** On claims that Lincoln was baptized by an associate of [Alexander Campbell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Campbell_\(clergyman\) "Alexander Campbell (clergyman)"), see Martin, Jim (1996). ["The secret baptism of Abraham Lincoln"](https://web.archive.org/web/20121019204330/http://www.acu.edu/sponsored/restoration_quarterly/archives/1990s/vol_38_no_2_contents/martin.html). *Restoration Quarterly*. **38** (2). Archived from [the original](http://www.acu.edu/sponsored/restoration_quarterly/archives/1990s/vol_38_no_2_contents/martin.html) on October 19, 2012. Retrieved May 27, 2012.
14. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-376)** Lincoln wrote to Thurlow Weed on March 4, 1865, "on the recent Inaugeral \[*sic*] Address. I expect the latter to wear as well as—perhaps better than—any thing I have produced...."
15. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-402)** While the book *Rating The Presidents: A Ranking of U.S. Leaders, From the Great and Honorable to the Dishonest and Incompetent* acknowledges that polls have rated Lincoln among the top presidents since 1948, the authors find him to be among the two best presidents, along with Franklin Delano Roosevelt.[\[387\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-401)
